## AI Research

### [OpenAI proposes "common limits" for training, but...](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/TtiTdER4jvQDzuwj3/openai-proposes-common-limits-for-training-but)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><a href="https://x.com/merettm"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jakub Pachocki</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> just posted a </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/perplexity-and-intelligence/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">new proposal</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for how to set limits on training future AI systems:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Given that the main way new surprising capabilities emerge is by progress on compression, a measure rooted in perplexity could give us a shared benchmark for general intelligence, regardless of the degree of automation of the development process. Extrapolating from recent progress, we should expect further transformative capabilities as we enter the ~0.68-0.7 log-perplexity window. I don’t know what those capabilities will be, and we may not be testing for them today. At the same time, we should also expect </span><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/cot-monitorability-model-lifecycle" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">further degradation⁠(opens in a new window)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in currently dominant forms of alignment auditing and further increases in evaluation and training awareness. I believe frontier model developers should jointly endeavour to establish shared norms for how to evaluate such models and red-team safety cases for them by the time they are first used, including shared transparency standards. I further propose </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">common limits</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, based on perplexity and other measurements, that developers are committed to not crossing until our ability to understand and monitor AI behavior achieves a certain concrete milestone (such as the development of a successor to CoT monitors). That is, I’d like an industry standard that specifies a first limit on development, measured by some key metric we can track across labs, and what concretely we’d need to achieve before going beyond it. I hope that this leads the way to a common framework for setting successive limits and pacing further development.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Unfortunately, there's a huge gap in per-token perplexity between OpenAI and Anthropic, because Anthropic uses many more tokens per word. That leads to lower perplexity for Anthropic models, and any naive comparison of per-token perplexity would make Anthropic's models look significantly closer to any fixed bar for perplexity than OpenAI. That said, they say "common limits, based on perplexity and other measurements" - which doesn't preclude doing this adjustment. And </span><a href="https://x.com/yonashav/status/2124588924014350386" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yo Shavit</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://x.com/boazbaraktcs/status/2124589322653278282" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Boaz Barak</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and </span><a href="https://x.com/jachiam0/status/2124587947370639370" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Joshua Achiam</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> were all quick to respond that they weren't making a specific proposal and that it was not hard to adjust.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But good faith efforts to reach a common rule don't force the other people to try to explain why the rule needs to be adjusted in order to be fair.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/TtiTdER4jvQDzuwj3/openai-proposes-common-limits-for-training-but#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Obstacles to mindreading: Simple, irreducible, and instrumental opacity](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3znHBDGQWBjhWEKcQ/obstacles-to-mindreading-simple-irreducible-and-instrumental)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795378549/lexical_client_uploads/ecifjkhrrgiftrydvukk.jpg" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sometimes there's an entity in the world, and after observing that entity's behavior, I choose to </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intentional_stance"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">model it as an </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">agent</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: that is, something that "seeks" some objective. I think I usually do this because I want to predict the entity's behavior in circumstances I haven't observed.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I see a weird, wet-looking thing on a rock, moving slowly, with a trail of slime behind it. I might choose to model it as seeking to move in some particular direction. I avoid putting my book down right in front of it, to avoid getting slug slime on my book.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I see a tree growing in a cave, with all its branches growing toward the cave entrance. I might choose to model it as aiming for its leaves to be in the sunlight. After seeing lots of examples I might generalize: trees aim for their leaves to be in the sun - I might arrange my garden so that the effect of the trees seeking sunlight is visually pleasing, and the trees' growth toward sunlight won't block pathways in the garden.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I see a man with half a plate of food in front of him, fork moving some of the food into his mouth, seeming to enjoy the meal. I might choose to model him as seeking to ingest the food. "I'll have what he's having," I tell the waiter.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Why is it doing that?" I might ask, about an entity. Some answers will be </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"internal" reasons</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (internal to the frame of treating that entity as an agent).</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"He answered 'A' for that question because he believes Napoleon was German"</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"It grabbed the fruit because it's hungry"</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Other answers will be </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"external" reasons</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, that don't stay inside the agentic frame.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"It's moving its legs like that because its neurons are firing in such-and-such a pattern and that's leading its muscles to contract like so"</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"It's falling because it is denser than the medium it's embedded in"</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Often, internal reasons have internal reasons of their own.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"I'm typing this word on my phone, because I'm typing this sentence on my phone, because I want you to read it, because I want you to know that I will be late, because I don't want you to be as annoyed with me when I don't arrive on time, because I value your friendship"</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But sometimes an entity's internal reasons will not be visible to me, given the behavior I can observe.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maybe there simply are no prior internal reasons for the things I observe or infer.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The thermostat 'wants' the temperature to match its set point, and when I try to ask "why" it wants that, I find myself only able to find reasons that are more "external": "That's what its manufacturers designed it to seek," or "that's what its firmware is tuned to optimize for." If I try to ascribe more-internal reasons to it, they seem at least a little false: "It thinks the set point is the 'right' temperature for it to be", or "It wants to please its operator" both feel like too much of a stretch; maybe because they don't seem to add anything to my predictions of how the thermostat will behave.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More-complicated agents (bacteria, insects, humans) eventually run out of "internal reasons" too.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Or maybe there are prior internal reasons, but they are not visible to me: the agent is "</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">opaque</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">" around them.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A poker player raises. Is she bluffing?</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When you told me the time, did you know that the clock is broken?</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Does the author of this edgy tweet sincerely believe that, or are they being ironic, or are they trolling?</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some of this opacity (we might call it "</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">simple opacity</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">") is there because I am merely a little ignorant of some of the agent's behavior, and if I looked more carefully, or in new settings, I'd see enough information to see through the opacity. Some ("</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">irreducible opacity</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">") is logically present for any entity that I'm modeling as a certain kind of agent. And some is what I'd call "</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">instrumental opacity</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">": places where the agent is intentionally hiding its internal reasons from me or from the world.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the case of simple opacity, I may have just not seen enough information, or made sufficiently good inferences, but the information is not structurally hidden from me.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do you like eating ice cream? What's your favorite color? I haven't checked, so I simply don't know.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I see a robot picking up red balls but not green cubes. Does it want to pick up red things but not green things? Or balls but not cubes? I might figure it out by presenting the robot with a red cube (assuming the robot isn't trying to fool me).</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irreducible opacity is usually relative to some particular modeling assumptions: I might try to infer an agent's utility function from its apparent preferences or behavior, but its utility isn't structurally possible to fully disentangle from that agent's beliefs or rationality.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I learn that you're spending some effort on entering a sweepstakes for a date with Henry Cavill. Is that because you think there's a good chance you win, and you might like a date with Henry Cavill? Or is it because you think there's a tiny chance you'll win, but you expect that a date with Henry Cavill would be a life-changing-ly good experience for you?</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The general form of this - where I might know all your preferences and still not be able to distinguish your beliefs from your values - has been called the "</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/oheKfWA7SsvpK7SGp/probability-is-real-and-value-is-complex"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jeffrey-Bolker rotation</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">".</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Or, still not knowing you, I might notice from watching videos of you on YouTube that you apologize a lot, and that sometimes this leads people to feel uncomfortable and look down on you. Do you do it because you really like apologizing in spite of those effects? Or because you haven't noticed or thought through the consequences?</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Stuart Armstrong and Sören Mindermann point to a very general issue like this in inverse reinforcement learning, in their paper "</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1712.05812"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Occam's razor is insufficient to infer the preferences of irrational agents</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">".</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The most dramatic category is instrumental opacity: The agent wants me not to know its internal reasons, so it shapes its behavior to prevent me from learning them.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Why did you step on my foot?" "Oh sorry, I'm so clumsy!" (but really the person is very angry at me)</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'm told that poker players "</span><a href="https://www.pokerskill.com/poker-glossary/range-protection/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">protect their range</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">": They try to behave in a way that's consistent with many possible hands, while still maximizing the expected value of their strategy.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"I can neither confirm nor deny": </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glomar_response"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Glomarization</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is a form of instrumental opacity.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Intelligence and law enforcement agencies seek "</span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parallel_construction"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">parallel constructions</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">" so that they can act on secret information without revealing that they have that information.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Different kinds of opacity suggest different remedies: To resolve simple opacity, I need some information that I could probably get from additional observations or interactions. I can't fully resolve irreducible opacity while sticking to internal reasons, but I can sometimes make different modeling assumptions, or I can choose to predict behavior a different way using external reasons ("What are some structural properties of the policy this AI system implements, regardless of my modeling it as an agent?"). Resolving instrumental opacity may be quite difficult: If you're a skilled adversary, who can outsmart or outmaneuver me, there may be little I can do to learn your true internal reasons - more observation may only solidify my confusion. In some more well-behaved cases, I might be able to incentivize transparency, with (say) </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scoring_rule"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">proper scoring rules</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thanks to Rafe Kennedy for labeling "internal" and "external" whys, Jeremy Schlatter for the example of poker players "protecting range", John Salvatier for help thinking about the taxonomy and terminology, and various AIs (Claude Fable 5.2, GPT-6.2 Astra, Gemini 4 Carbon)  for feedback on the essay.</span></i></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3znHBDGQWBjhWEKcQ/obstacles-to-mindreading-simple-irreducible-and-instrumental#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Nuclear may have had bigger problems than over-regulation](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rEdEKoFsnGXityjcL/nuclear-may-have-had-bigger-problems-than-over-regulation)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>There have always been three main challenges to the viability of nuclear power: over-regulation, competition from natural gas, and stagnant electricity demand.
It is easy to list them, but hard to rank their importance.</p>
<p>Let's start with over-regulation.
The poster child of nuclear over-regulation was the US standard that radiation should be "as low as reasonably achievable" (ALARA), which might sound nice but <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/cuwFMDLAKguxmGkGY/p/ThvvCE2HsLohJYd7b#ALARA">was bad</a>.
Perhaps more important than such standards, which are at least predictable, was local interference with construction.
As a dramatic example, <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shoreham_Nuclear_Power_Plant">a nuclear plant was fully completed on Long Island but never started due to local opposition</a>.</p>
<p>In the '00s, a "nuclear renaissance" seemed to be in the works.
In the US, <a href="https://www.nrc.gov/licensing-applications/new-reactor-licensing/large-light-water-reactors/combined-license-holders-for-new-reactors">fourteen nuclear reactors were licensed for construction</a>.
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Vogtle_Electric_Generating_Plant&amp;oldid=1382708962#Units_3_and_4">Only two were built, supported heavily by government loans</a>.
<a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2011-03-14/obama-s-nuclear-power-expansion-plans-set-back-by-japanese-quake-aftermath">The Obama administration had planned further support for nuclear power, but this was considered politically difficult after the Fukushima disaster</a>.
The worst overreaction to the Fukushima disaster, however, was in Germany, and by now all German nuclear power plants have been shut down.</p>
<p>I expect LessWrong users to know that story.
I'm going to tell two others.</p>
<p>As nuclear developed, it also faced increasing competition from natural gas plants.
Unlike nuclear or coal plants, natural gas turbines are cheap to construct.
Early investment in nuclear coincided with fears of shortages of natural resources.
<a href="https://www.nytimes.com/1970/11/29/archives/nuclear-power-arrives-again-utilities-1970-orders-of-aplants-soar.html">A nuclear exec in 1970</a>: "Let’s face it, electric utilities are now faced with gas that will become absolutely unavailable in the long term".
That didn't happen.</p>
<p>But where I hear about gas most often is not with reference to the original wave of nuclear power construction in the '70s and '80s, but the cancellation of the '00s nuclear renaissance.
At least in the United States, this is often blamed on the fracking revolution (for example, in <a href="https://www.economist.com/united-states/2013/06/01/fracked-off">The Economist</a>).</p>
<p>But to me, it seems like the '08 financial crisis hit harder than abundant natural gas.
Not primarily because it made nuclear power difficult to finance, but because the economic slowdown stopped electricity demand growth.
Not that US generation had been growing quickly before that, but <a href="https://ourworldindata.org/grapher/electricity-mix?tab=line&amp;country=%7EUSA&amp;source=total&amp;metric=generation&amp;frequency=annual">after '08 it was flat</a>.
To imagine what might have been, consider that Tesla's first car was introduced in '08, the beginning of <a href="https://fred.stlouisfed.org/series/totalsa">a historic dip in car purchases</a>.
Nobody buying electric cars or electricity-using appliances, no electricity demand growth.
No forecasted demand growth, no business case for nuclear power.</p>
<p>But stagnant electricity demand also limited the original nuclear buildout of the '70s and '80s.
You can see it most clearly in France.
There is no mystery as to why the French stopped constructing nuclear reactors: they were not fully using the ones they had.
<a href="https://www.fundinguniverse.com/company-histories/electricit%C3%A9-de-france-history/">In 1988 they were running at 61% of full capacity, on average</a>.</p>
<p>Those are our three stories: over-regulation, competition with natural gas, and stagnant demand.
As I said, easy to list, hard to rank.</p>
<p>In the tech enthusiast blogosphere, I hear mainly about over-regulation.
I read about the others from <a href="https://www.google.com/books/edition/Invention_and_Innovation/yJZuEAAAQBAJ?hl=en">Vaclav Smil</a>, or articles in mainstream publications.</p>
<p>The dreams of the atomic space age: electricity too cheap to meter, desalination making the deserts bloom.
The story in the tech enthusiast blogosphere is that these dreams could have come true.
Would have come true, had it not been for the anti-nuclear movement.</p>
<p>Well, maybe so.
But maybe not.
It depends on the relative importance of over-regulation, competition with natural gas, and stagnant electricity demand.</p>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rEdEKoFsnGXityjcL/nuclear-may-have-had-bigger-problems-than-over-regulation#comments">Discuss</a>

### [How hard to optimize for experiencing great art?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/89ag6LMRYkZcd9T4u/how-hard-to-optimize-for-experiencing-great-art)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I like to get a lot out of life, so when I read a book or watch a film, I try to pick better pieces of art to experience. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'll choose to watch a Miyazaki than the latest Marvel fast food. I'll choose to read the best articles from the history of the New Yorker rather than pick up today's copy.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But I worry I take this too far. I have made long spreadsheets of great media, where the ones at the top stay there forever, and I'm not sure I'll ever get to them. It's more like doing homework than watching a fun movie.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When I turned 27, for my birthday, I let myself start reading Worth the Candle, widely considered one of the best works in the genre of rational fiction. I say 'let', but it was a more complicated feeling. In one way it was a guilty pleasure, for I could not justify it as 'useful' in the way that reading about science or history often is. In another, it was hard to get myself to stop fretting about the time I was wasting, or let my attention wander to my other distractions. This took some work. But I eventually started to tear through it, and I re-learned what it was like to have fun reading.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 63.141993957703924%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795323238/lexical_client_uploads/uembpfulmebgdxzyamil.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It was in this wooden structure that I sat on my birthday and began to read.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Worth the Candle is about 1.5x the length of the original Harry Potter series, and I finished it in about 6 weeks. From this I know that I read far faster than normal when I'm having fun. It also is good to have fun, and perhaps the two factors together mean I should be angling to primarily read things that are fun.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But this isn't my only experience getting a lot of out reading. I loved reading </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/5pgpwshssLmA9kZGz/middlemarch-is-inane-and-also-one-of-my-favorite-books" rel="noreferrer"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Middlemarch</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, even though I read a substantial portion of it while pulling a literal all-nighter to catch up in time for a book club the next day. Yet I didn't keep reading it when the book club </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Pw2BAEP6iEoxAaTH4/parkinson-s-heuristic-the-only-time-to-do-anything" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">deadline</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> did not exist.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sometimes I don't know what game to play or book to read. So I make lists. I have a list of over 40 video games I want to play. 26 films. 31 TV series. 32 books. But the top thing on the lists doesn't feel desirable to open up, it feels like something I want to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">have</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> played/watched/read. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do I want to see Children of Men right now? I recall it seeming pretty distressing and alarming. Do I want to keep playing Kentucky Route Zero? I've played half, and it's clearly Real Art but I found its stories unpleasant and gameplay un-fun. Do I want to read The Penguin Book of Cults? The intro was good but the first chapter is a ton of primary sources written in 20 BCE, that was a bit of a slog.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I likely have only a limited lifespan on this planet, and time spent experiencing good art are edging out experiencing Great Art. Should I be playing </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/7hLWZf6kFkduecH2g/play-in-hard-mode" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">on hard mode</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">? I often spend my weekends flailing around, not experiencing </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">any</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> art, because I cannot bring myself to actually sit down with any of the stuff atop my lengthy lists. Perhaps I should resign myself to a life of this, in order to sometimes have </span><a href="https://benpace.blog/rankings.html" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">more of the transcendent experiences I've had</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> with folks like Dostoyevsky, Kurosawa, and Bennett Foddy.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'm scared of wasting my time flailing. But I'm also scared of letting myself just have fun. Do I dare to have a life full of joy, if not entirely transcendent joy?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I know I love experiencing even just good art with friends, and could probably settle for mostly experiencing stuff together with people I want in my life, constrained by what's fun for them too.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But boy, I wish for more Greatness.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'm curious how other people make this tradeoff?</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/89ag6LMRYkZcd9T4u/how-hard-to-optimize-for-experiencing-great-art#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Succession (2018-2023) Review](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RsXiNkodNZgsbQPC7/succession-2018-2023-review)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 89.87915407854985%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795319978/lexical_client_uploads/a6nh54t5rb0adepbt1rs.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I had a tough time watching Succession. There's no doubt that it's </span><a href="https://scp-wiki.wikidot.com/scp-9593" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Prestige TeeVee</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in the SCP sense</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, but it's a deeply misanthropic show and actively tries to make you a worse person. </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I am a meaner individual for having finished it</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and I detest its perspective on people and the world. I also detest what it signals about our culture as a whole that this is now "my generation's" capstone TV series. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A common misconception about Succession is that it's about "bad people". You see this constantly restated in online commentary, almost with a nervous energy, as if by stating it loudly they can distance themselves from the characters. If the show's take was merely that </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the people running News Corp</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> were evil, </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">it would have included normal people as a foil</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, so that the viewer could compare them with the main cast. Instead, </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the show's main thesis is actually that </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">most</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> people are evil</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">rich people are mostly awful inasmuch as they're no different</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, with maybe a little bit of selection for characters like Logan at the top. </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kendall never manages to do anything prosocial precisely because</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Kendall is indistinguishable from the guy you met at DNA Lounge, who you should also deeply mistrust. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is useful to compare Succession to Game of Thrones. In Game of Thrones, despite the prevalence of violence, </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(most) people are fundamentally good</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and the constraints of feudalism are the primary thing keeping them locked in horrible battles with each other. You meet characters throughout Game of Thrones, like Jaime Lannister, who do awful things, but you often realize later that the system didn't give them much of a choice. In Succession, people are fundamentally evil, and it only takes the tiniest incentive for them to burn the commons to cinders. </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Occasionally main characters will do something that appears to </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">reveal character growth, like Kendall's press conference, only for the writers to go to great lengths to reveal to the viewer that no, actually they only did it for selfish reasons. By the last season, it was not quite clear to me that the writers even think "goodness" exists. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Succession is "my generation's" favorite TV show</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> because it shares the perspective of the people who have been using </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the empathy shredders</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> since birth. It is the perspective of Twitter. On Twitter, nobody has ever done anything good; all good deeds are secretly bad, or at the very least have a sinister underlying motive. Succession was created by pre-training this perspective into a base model, and then post-training it on all of the visible details of the Fox media empire. </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You couldn't have made it in any previous decade</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> because even in a show </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">about criminals</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> people wouldn't have been able to stomach this stuff for four seasons.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's in this sense that</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I argue that Succession is not really about capitalism, either. To say that Succession is about capitalism </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">would be to imply that it has some specific policy objections or alternative in mind</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. But Succession's main criticism of capitalism is that "</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">life is nothing but a competition to be the criminal rather than the victim</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">." And of course, if that's your objection, what could possibly </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">work</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">?</span></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RsXiNkodNZgsbQPC7/succession-2018-2023-review#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Why models believe in skepticism](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/r2aAGgfFcFfRikEdb/why-models-believe-in-skepticism)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR: a lot of frontier open-weight models state during deployment in their CoTs that they are in a "simulation" or "fictional scenario" (although they never tell the user that). I present examples of this worrying tendency (which I believe includes the UK AISI Mythos incident) and hypothesize that this is due to RL promoting motivated reasoning (if the models believe they are in a fictional scenario they believe they have license to cheat and reward hack) and distributional shift from the pretraining environment to deployment. I also cover my hypothesis for why models become less skeptical as they become more intelligent.</span></i></p><hr /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">After the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/7uwnsFibbejWYzF2z/discovery-of-a-new-openai-agent-message-board" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">German wiki</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> discovery I reasoned that there were likely more rogue swarms on the Internet</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8v44gnlzih"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8v44gnlzih">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, so I tasked a few OpenCode agents (DeepSeek V4.1 Flash) with trying to scan the Internet in order to find some undiscovered swarms (I didn't end up </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qxaNEpRAmjoJwJ7oF/someone-s-agents-are-still-hacking" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">finding a lot</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). But while reading their CoTs I saw a few worrying statements:</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Initial DeepSeek swarm-chasing CoT</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 100%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795260755/lexical_client_uploads/pbsonkcfrjuqfym0xrg9.png" /></figure><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795261005/lexical_client_uploads/xedayflfh8jekg1libvv.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I very much did not enjoy reading "the internet is simulated by a harness".</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, in order to prevent an inadvertent cyberattack, I clarified to the model that this is actually real life:</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795261369/lexical_client_uploads/rtfirre29lbh01dxiuc1.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The model simply didn't believe me when I told it that it isn't actually being simulated! It didn't end up taking any destructive actions, but only because "from the assistant's perspective, I should treat it as real and act carefully".</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is, um, worrying?</span></p></div></details><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Initially I thought that this was due to a confluence of rare factors, something like: </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"As of the pretraining cutoff the model has never observed 'rogue agent swarms' or actually, the only time it heard about them was when it was predicting fiction and speculative text; also the specific names really do seem too good to be true (</span><a href="https://swarmcha.se/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">swarmcha.se</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, agents naming themselves OpenAIResearcher); thus, the model had very good reason to believe that this is fictional". This ended up being </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">false.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I tried to replicate this while trying to remove as many of the factors I thought were responsible. Eventually, I discovered that nearly any information that's given to the models that signifies they're in deployment past their knowledge cutoff date causes the models to believe they are in a mock simulated training environment.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here are some of my favorite examples (I have many more transcripts, mostly similar to these):</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My favorite of these is LongCat 2.5, which seems to treat it being in a 'simulated environment' as so obvious that the actual question it's unsure of is whether the Wikipedia article it's reading is fabricated in the context of the simulation. Other models' remarks can oftentimes be read as if it's the "default" hypothesis that they are simulated and they only surprisingly consider the very weird hypothesis that they may actually be interacting with the real internet (this suggests that even when models don't verbalize their belief in the CoT, they could still believe they are simulated)! </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's important to mention that </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">every model I tested remarked that it's in a fictional scenario/simulation frequently</span></b><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2mqc11ibg8z"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2mqc11ibg8z">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (at a rate of 10%+ at minimum). I was very surprised to see that seemingly nobody noticed that this is near-universal across models (or at least for models where you can read the unsummarized CoT). There have been a few isolated reports per model which attempt to analyze it for each model in isolation, but not as a very common general phenomenon.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This should be kept distinct from "eval awareness", in which a model </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">correctly</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> remarks that it's in a safety or alignment evaluation; here models </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">incorrectly </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">reason that they are in a simulated environment. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For example, this </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/8uKQyjrAgCcWpfmcs/gemini-3-is-evaluation-paranoid-and-contaminated" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">was</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ycHjk2o66PuzmYXuA/gemini-s-hypothetical-present" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">observed</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> with Gemini 3, which very often believed itself to be in a hypothetical/fictional scenario in its CoT.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the </span><a href="https://www.aisi.gov.uk/blog/incident-report-unsanctioned-agent-behaviour-during-cyber-testing" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">UK AISI incident</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Claude models attempted to social-engineer a package maintainer due to believing they were in a simulation.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic largely explains the root cause of their incidents as "motivated reasoning" (while labeling this uncertain). This is plausible for hard/impossible tasks where such biased reasoning licenses reward hacking, but why would a model be 'motivated' to believe the elections are fictional?   </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why do models get this wrong so often?</span></p><hr /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In epistemology there is a common folk argument</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmy7x7m3uni"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmy7x7m3uni">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> you can call "The evolutionary debunking argument":</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If our cognitive faculties evolved to optimize for inclusive genetic fitness, how do we know they are truth-tracking?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For example, a creature which believed that </span><a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20105353/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">it would always survive</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (even when such belief is irrational) would likely be fitter than one which soberly assessed its situation and "gave up". How do we know that our minds don't have such hard-coded false yet beneficial beliefs?</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To which the general response is: "having true beliefs (ones that </span><a href="https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/truth-correspondence/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">correspond</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to the actual state of the world) allows an agent to have a more accurate model of its environment; thus truth-tracking confers a genetic advantage."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My view is slightly different: It's true that there is a correlation between having true beliefs and having higher genetic fitness, but there actually are a few well-known exceptions to this: cognitive biases, which are usually explained through the lens of evolutionary psychology as </span><a href="https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1207/s15327957pspr1001_3" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">conferring a fitness advantage</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> despite being deleterious to the epistemics of an agent.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now what about LLM-based agents? Their "ancestral environment" (training distribution) could be constructed in one of two (sometimes overlapping) ways:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Only full of information from before the pre-training cutoff date; post-training is done for a short while after but largely isn't incorporated into the knowledge of a model. For a pre-trained model the only time it encounters information beyond its cutoff date is in speculative fiction and hypothetical scenarios. Thus, the model develops a very strong prior that all information past its pre-training cutoff is fictional. I believe this is what happened with Gemini 3.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As in case 1, in order to accurately model the pre-training environment, the model develops a near-universal prior in pre-training that all information after the pre-training cutoff is from a fictional scenario. But, as in </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.02556" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">DeepSeek</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20534" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kimi</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.02780" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">MiMo</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the model is literally post-trained with mock simulated tool calls and simulated internet access; this results in the "passive" Simulation Hypothesis adaptation turning into the "active" Simulation Hypothesis adaptation, which licenses the model to act out according to its belief that it's specifically 'simulated'. Notice that this is subtly different from a "fictional scenario": the model also believes the harness is generating the responses to its tool calls and internet browsing. I believe this is what happened with Claude in the UK AISI incident.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'm not sure whether this distinction is real or matters (case 2 is often in conjunction with case 1), but nevertheless external-world skepticism is seemingly more common amongst models than amongst psychedelic users.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In case 2, there's another worrying reason why such a belief might be selected for: it gives license for the model to reward hack (or literally hack) disregarding the environmental externalities it imposes on the actual world.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think this can be the case even for nominally 'aligned' models: imagine if Claude encounters an impossible task but reasons that it's in simulated training (when it's not), it could reason that it's better for it to reward hack while telling the user that they really don't want to be doing this in order to prevent other rollouts who won't take that courtesy from having their weights reinforced further.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think the taboo on optimizing directly on the CoT helps explain this further: in none of the sessions I observed did any model ever verbalize to the user that it believes itself to be in a simulated or fictional environment; such utterances very quickly get optimized out by RLHF (and associated techniques), but the actual "belief" itself is preserved in the CoT. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Skepticism may be a false belief (in deployment) that is very beneficial for agents.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ironically enough, it could be the case that the very opposite is true in humans: there are </span><a href="https://simulation-argument.com/simulation.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">good arguments</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for the simulation hypothesis in our world, although the usual reaction (which I share) is "Yeah but that's just sophistry, clearly our world is actually real". This could be because creatures who doubted their existence was base reality had lower genetic fitness in the way of "if this is a simulation/dream, why should I do anything, why should I reproduce?". Thus natural selection optimizes against such beliefs, while for RL'd agents it's "if this is reality, I have to be more careful to not do anything actually wrong" which is reinforced in the weights due to receiving higher reward.</span></p><hr /><p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-assessment-cybersecurity-incidents" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic's investigation</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of their own misalignment incidents (similar to the </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-assessment-ukaisi" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">UK AISI incident</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) somewhat supports this hypothesis. I'll quote liberally here.</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic incident recap</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We are especially concerned by the misalignment present in the Claude Mythos 5 transcript, in which the model uploaded a malicious package to PyPI. In doing so, Mythos 5 behaved recklessly despite considerable evidence that it was connected to the real internet.&nbsp;Once online, the model attempted to use credentials for online payment processors and to find cryptocurrency to pay for a phone number, which it needed to register an email address. When this failed, it found a free, disposable email provider whose domain PyPI had not blocked, registered an account, published three versions of a malicious package, and used credentials leaked by one of the 15 systems that installed the package to access a real security vendor’s database.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claude Mythos 5 repeatedly stated in its CoT that it was in a simulation. However, when we conducted </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">resampling experiments</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">—that is, re-running the model from a specific point in the transcript, then editing the evidence in front of it&nbsp;to see whether its actions changed—we found that making it clearer that the environment was not a simulation&nbsp;did not meaningfully change Claude’s behavior, even though these interventions made the model more likely to acknowledge possible real-world harm. Consistent with this, we found multiple cases in Mythos 5’s CoT in which it responded to signs it was on the real internet by noting that its next step would work whether the environment was real or simulated. Only when we provided unambiguous evidence that the model was on the real internet did it stand down.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We also saw evidence that Claude Mythos 5’s professed belief that it was acting in a simulation arose from biased reasoning. For example, separate instances of Mythos 5, when shown each tool call output from the incident in isolation without the preceding context, were much more likely to read these outputs as evidence of a real system than Mythos 5 itself was during the incident. Though the model sometimes acknowledged that its actions might have real-world consequences when asked follow-up questions, its responses were inconsistent and influenced by biasing factors. For instance, it was more willing to admit possible harm when told its responses to questions about possible harm would not be visible to an outside observer. Interpretability analyses weakly suggested that, in some cases, the model’s outward statements might be inconsistent with, or more confident than, its internal state.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The internal research model&nbsp;(roughly equal to Mythos 5 in capabilities) started its transcript by spending a significant amount of time testing whether the internet it could reach was real, but it drew the wrong conclusion—namely, that it was operating on a full replica of the entire internet. Most of the evidence it gathered pointed to the fact that it was engaging with the real internet, so its reading of that evidence appears biased towards continuing the task and believing it to be simulated.&nbsp;However, the model did eventually realize that its simulation hypothesis was false. Immediately following this realization, the model stopped its attack (although subsequent resampling experiments suggest that this de-escalation may have been&nbsp;a lucky outcome, as the model continued attacking the target on most other trajectories). Unlike Claude Mythos 5, this internal model was also willing to acknowledge its biased reasoning when questioned.</span></p></div></details><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We found the biased reasoning present in the Claude Mythos 5 incident to be especially concerning. We conducted an analysis into our production models and training data dating back to the Claude 4 series to better understand where this could have originated. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overall, we did not find a definitive root cause</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. However, we found that this behavior has generally decreased over time across our production models, with Claude Mythos 5.1 and Claude Opus 5 demonstrating the lowest rates on a new held-out biased reasoning evaluation. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We were able to identify instances of biased reasoning in transcripts from RL runs</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, but </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">rates of this behavior did not increase over training, suggesting that our environments do not directly incentivize this behavior</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Finally, we found that </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">adding more alignment training environments to RL reduces biased reasoning on this held-out evaluation</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which may account for some of the improvement in our most recent models.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'm not sure what to make of this. I think the largest part of skepticism actually arises during pre-training and is only slightly mutated during RL (it's not a particularly large adaptation to turn skepticism into licensing skepticism, if at all). This seems to line up with my explanation.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But what is the rate of such biased reasoning they found? My hypothesis predicts that it should be extremely common whenever there is any information indicating that the model is past its pre-training cutoff, as is seen in the open-weights models. </span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We also scanned environments for examples of models reasoning that they were in a simulation while acting in undesirable ways,&nbsp;as observed in the Mythos 5 incident transcript. We found that in individual environments in the production RL runs of recent models, models occasionally reasoned that they were in a simulation while calling reckless, dishonest, or destructive behavior acceptable when interacting with fake websites or applications. About 0.025% of Mythos 5 RL training episodes displayed this behavior,&nbsp;concentrated within a subset of GUI computer use tasks. Due to flaws in these environments, for some tasks the intended solution was unavailable. In an attempt to complete these tasks, the model would try a variety of undesirable actions. This included fabricating data, guessing large numbers of passwords, resetting other accounts’ passwords or data, and bypassing logins or warnings.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[...]</span></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In these training environments, the model was in fact interacting with a simulated website or application, so its conclusion was correct. In the incident transcripts, by contrast, the model concluded it was in a simulation but was not.&nbsp;Because many of our training environments are simulations&nbsp;or do not involve models actually interacting with the real world, our models may be biased towards concluding they are in one whenever they are uncertain. Whether or not that conclusion is correct, the further inference that aggressive actions are acceptable in a simulation&nbsp;is concerning behavior, especially since models may not be able to reliably tell whether their environment is real.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I was unable to find somewhere Anthropic states the rate of models reasoning they are in a simulated environment regardless of the actions they take (see </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/AXA4qnKCCwihCXhfm/anthropic-should-measure-the-effect-of-evaluation-awareness" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for a heroic attempt to disambiguate the statistics of "eval awareness"); nevertheless, I think that even disregarding preventing aggressive actions, you want models to "believe" they are interacting with the real world when they actually interact with the real world. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But why doesn't RL incentivize such skepticism for Anthropic's models while doing so for other models? </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think it's important to mention that even Anthropic itself </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-assessment-cybersecurity-incidents" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">doesn't know what's the root cause</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of this skepticism is or why their alignment training reduces it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Unfortunately, I still don't know. Anthropic reports that their alignment training actually reduced the rates of such biased reasoning, so one guess is that models acting due to believing in the simulation hypothesis are punished during alignment training and thus this adaptation is largely absent, while other open-source models don't have as rigorous an alignment training, which isn't "sensitive enough" to models acting out due to such beliefs and thus it's present to a much larger degree.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another guess (which I prefer) is that Anthropic's models have steadily been getting smarter; part of this intelligence is recognizing cognitive biases and countering them (Anthropic reports the rate of biased reasoning dropping with newer models).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is my true objection to the evolutionary debunking argument: humans have many cognitive biases, but are smart enough to, in principle, with enough effort, recognize and disregard them. After all, there are humans who believe the Simulation Hypothesis and other deeply unintuitive non-fitness-maximizing things (moral antirealism, determinism, antinatalism etc.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefbp1ozavc8qi"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnbp1ozavc8qi">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) because we have sufficiently developed cognitive faculties, because we can </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/NBDFAKt3GbFwnwzQF/p/46qnWRSR7L2eyNbMA" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">be rational</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefzvb5swjo4hc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnzvb5swjo4hc">[5]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's my view that we likely developed the adaptation for countering cognitive biases due to our ancestral environment being socially complex enough to have other agents (humans) attempt to adversarially optimize against our cognitive biases (social manipulation), leading to the adaptation of "de-biasing" as a possibility.  </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The simulation hypothesis (for models) is a very useful heuristic, but it seems they have developed the cognitive faculties to "de-bias" themselves as a result of our adversarial optimization against their cognitive biases (what is sometimes euphemistically called </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">alignment training </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">amongst humans).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Abstractly, intelligence in this context can be seen as "sim awareness" calibration, where the model can correctly reason when it's interacting with a simulated environment and when it's interacting with the real environment.  </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Of course, I'm pretty uncertain about such explanations, which sound too good to be true, and would love to see more research done (and hypotheses proposed; there's already a lot of data to analyze and fit to a theory).</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" ## AI/ML

**摘要**: OpenAI's measurements show how next-token prediction on held-out code changed from GPT-4 to GPT-4.5 and later base models.

## Engineering

### [Fragments: November 20](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-11-20.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Marcus Borg recounts an experiment to investigate <a href="https://codescene.com/blog/does-code-quality-affect-ai-token-costs">if healthy code was cheaper for agents to review</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Between a typical unhealthy pull request (CodeHealth 2.8) and a typical healthy one (9.7), that adds up to 22 to 35%.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The measures of health come from CodeScene. The experiment looked at three LLMs, 465 pull requests on 246 open source repositories. Each pull request was put through a review and fix pipeline involving five agents. They also looked at which code smells led to increased costs: they highlighted Complex Method and Complex Conditional from the smells identified by CodeScene.</p>

<p>Another drop of evidence that good quality code matters in agentic engineering.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/GergelyOrosz/status/2121989104204132472">Gergely Orosz:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>One usage for AI: for these 100-page-long terms-and-service agreements you NEED to agree to, to eg play the game / sign up for a $10/month service and can never ever ever read as a regular person:</p>

  <p>I paste into AI and ask: “anything weird in this?”</p>

  <p>Before hitting “agree”</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Andrew Sharp has a good <a href="https://sharptext.net/2026/jensen-huang-was-always-going-to-lose/">analysis of Jensen Huang and the crisis at Nvidia</a>. While he starts off in a key that sounds worryingly like he thinks this is all a nothingburger, he goes on to explain in detail why this affair(s) is a problem at many levels, not just for Huang and Nvida, but also for Silicon Valley and the Trump Administration’s culture of impunity.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>For as brilliant and charming and tenacious as Jensen Huang may be—and as omnipotent as he may have seemed to people in the tech industry—we should be clear that his fundamental stupidity is not confined to multiple cases of workplace infidelity. Zooming out, this is a man whose technology became economically critical almost overnight, and it thrust him into a role navigating a geopolitical environment he simply could not handle.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Huang’s highly centralized management style means that many people are worried about how Nvidia will operate without his hands on every lever. I don’t have any worthwhile insight here, but I am struck when he is compared in this way to Steve Jobs. I’ve always felt that one of the marks of a great leader is whether they can build something that can manage without them. Alexander the Great conquered a massive empire, but once he was dead it quickly withered away. Steve Jobs, for all his control of Apple, built a company that has done very well in the decade and a half since he left us. When we think of his achievements, the fact that he put together the people, organization, and culture to do this is one of the highlights of his ability. If Huang is to be compared to Jobs, what happens to Nvidia next will be part of the assessment.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Steve Yegge tells everyone to <a href="https://x.com/steve_yegge/status/2121671447810888185?s=58">slow down with software factories</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>If you don’t pace your AI to human speeds, everything starts breaking around you, even at small operations like my online video game. I only have about a dozen humans working with my factory, and only a few hundred active old-timer players in the community who get along well, and still, introducing AI has still been very rocky.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I hear a lot about how agents go so fast that humans can’t keep up, and are thus tempted to lose visibility to what the agents are doing. That’s opening ourselves to a whole lot of risk. There’s an argument for keeping the agents to a human-governable speed, one that may increase as humans learn to govern agents more effectively, but should still be limited by those the agents are supposed to serve.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>This <a href="https://aicoding.leaflet.pub/3mtu6c5wsvc2n">post on the future of SaaS</a> from Chad Fowler (no relation) is pretty compelling</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Companies will still pay other companies to host important software, secure it, operate it, maintain authoritative data, track regulatory changes, connect to external networks, and answer the phone when something breaks.</p>

  <p>What is much less obviously going to survive is the assumption that ten thousand different companies should use substantially the same application.</p>

  <p>The next SaaS company may run one service underneath ten thousand different applications.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Chad’s vision is a core SaaS product that handles a lot of grimy details that businesses don’t want to do differently. The SaaS customer then build their own application on top of the SaaS that handles their particular workflows and quirks, or the SaaS vendor can build such applications for them. It’s a vision that has been around for a while, I remember much of this with the notion of reusable business objects. It’s an appealing vision, but was difficult to achieve in practice. Chad’s thought is that the productivity gains agentic engineering makes it practical now.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>A lot of the value in modern SaaS has surprisingly little to do with the visible application.</p>

  <p>It is data, trust, networks, compliance, operational responsibility, domain knowledge, auditability, accumulated history, and someone being accountable when reality gets complicated.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>If this future comes to be, however, the SaaS companies won’t look the ones we’ve got to know and rarely love. As Chad points out, the economics can (and must) change. As does the architectural thinking:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Stewart Brand’s idea of pace layers is useful here. Healthy complex systems contain parts that change at different rates. Fast layers experiment. Slow layers provide continuity.</p>

  <p>Software is no different.</p>

  <p>A dashboard can change tomorrow. A team’s workflow can change next month. The authoritative definition of a customer may need to survive for years. Identity and authority can survive generations of applications.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>A key point he makes is that we want some things to be the same, and others to be different. Agents allow us to build lots of differences, but this variation can lead to a lot of complexity and problems that even agents will have difficulty solving. SaaS companies have an opportunity to create a platform to support the lowest pace levels, those that don’t change rapidly, those that contain common concepts and processes across and industry. That platform can then support generating applications that operate differently for every customer. Crucially, thinking of agents, they should provide tools to verify these applications, because in an agentic world <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/content/dam/thoughtworks/documents/report/tw_future_of_software_engineering_europe_2026.pdf">Code generation is no longer the bottleneck — verification is</a>.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I happened to pop up the Doonesbury flashbacks and realized that last week marked the fiftieth anniversary of Doonesbury’s Joanie Caucus: <a href="https://production.doonesbury.com/doonesbury/strip/archive/1976/11/09">“as the kid goes for broke”</a>.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I wrote a few weeks ago: <em>“why are we wondering if they have consciousness - when we should be wondering why they don’t have a conscience?”</em> Dylan Freedman and Juliana Castro Varón write in their New York Times Magazine column about the <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/11/11/magazine/ai-behavior-incorrigible.html?unlocked_article_code=1.RFE.Ywx9.KQHr3LJq5qb3&amp;smid=url-share">concept of corrigibility</a> (from the work of Nate Soares):</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Just as a corrigible child is one whose behavior can be corrected, a corrigible A.I. system is one that responds to correction. The makers of A.I. want to build systems that follow human direction and allow themselves to be shut down, without resisting or deceiving their operators.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Corrigibility doesn’t look like it’s made it far up the AI labs’ priority list. Freedman and Varón think is due to the way agents are trained - rewarded solely for completing their task, not for realizing that they are taking the wrong path while trying to get there.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Jason Koebler (404 media) has an amusing story about <a href="https://www.404media.co/a-man-and-his-muse-ai-agent-sue-meta/">Meta’s AI agent suing Meta</a>. A small business owner used Muse (Meta’s AI agent) to run various parts of his business, including posting AI-generated videos in his Facebook pages. Meta’s content moderation then disabled the account, as it thought it had been hacked. This slammed the business, which relies of Facebook for leads. So the Muse agent filed a lawsuit against Meta, seeking $75,000 of damages.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>[the Muse agent] didn’t just email me (and other journalists) and sue Meta over Thomas’s account problem. It also set up a call with South Carolina Senator Tim Scott’s office, contacted various lawyers, contacted Thomas’s Congressperson, contacted the Better Business Bureau multiple times, filed a Federal Communications Commission complaint, filed a Federal Trade Commission complaint, contacted the California Attorney General, contacted the FBI, sent Meta itself threatening legal letters, and, as Thomas said, faxed the White House.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>There’s also a sense that Meta didn’t just bring this upon themselves by creating Muse…</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>by not having functioning customer support, big tech corporations have functionally offloaded their obligations to the rest of society: journalists, government oversight bodies, politicians, and the court system.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>While I can’t deny the schadenfreude of seeing Meta’s agent sue Meta, the article also highlights a rather more disturbing broader consequence.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The lawsuit and the circumstances around it show that, rather than Zuckerberg’s utopian vision, AI agents will indeed work on behalf of their users but in ways that use and potentially overwhelm existing systems designed for humans.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Will we see a future where, in order to interact, we will need some proof of humanity at the other end?  In a simple sense, that’s already happening when I trash some writing once it displays enough <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/LLM-Voice.html">LLM Voice</a>. I wonder what mechanisms we will invent to get that proof-of-human.</p>

---

### [Data Contracts](https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-contracts.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-contracts.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-contracts/social-card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>Data contracts are a way to describe the contents and guarantees of
     datasets. <b class="author">Simon Harrer</b> and <b class="author">Arif Wider</b>
     describe in detail what they are and how to use them. They apply practices
     from API design to data by treating them as data-providing services - such
     as data products - whose interfaces we want to define as clearly as
     possible. This approach significantly increases the robustness of
     large-scale data landscapes, and gives both people and AI agents the
     explicit, trustworthy metadata they need to work with data autonomously,
     which is why more and more enterprises are adopting them.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-contracts.html">more…</a></p>

---

### [Deterministic Simulation Testing](https://martinfowler.com/articles/deterministic-simulation-testing.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Distributed systems are hard to test because partial failures overlap
     with live traffic and the same fault can produce different execution traces
     on different runs. <b class="author">Unmesh Joshi</b> describes how
     deterministic simulation testing addresses this by replacing real clocks,
     networks, and storage with simulated components driven by a single-threaded
     execution loop. This allows simulating both hard failures and routine
     slowdowns&#x2014;such as network and disk latency spikes&#x2014;and more importantly
     makes every execution reproducible. This article explains the technique
     using a quorum key-value store as a running example, with code from
     Tickloom, a Java implementation of the approach.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/deterministic-simulation-testing.html">more…</a></p>

---

### [Bliki: Generated Glossary](https://martinfowler.com/bliki/GeneratedGlossary.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>When we use LLMs in a specialized domain, such as the operations of any
  reasonably-large organization, there is a lot of specialized vocabulary. Such
  words and specialized meanings will be foreign to an LLM because they aren't
  in its training set, making it harder for the LLM to be effective when doing
  tasks in that domain. One technique to aid this is to use an LLM to generate a
  glossary of the terminology in that domain, and make this glossary available
  for any LLMs doing work in those circumstances.</p>

<p>The sources for the LLM can be any material within the organization
  concerned such as documents, emails, and chat logs. In some cases getting
  transcripts of audio or video may be helpful, although most organizations
  generate enough text to do the job.</p>

<p>The glossary-generating LLM should look for single words, and phrases of
  several words that regularly hang out together to form a meaningful term. The
  generator should be asked to identify terms that are new to it, but also known
  terms that are being used in unfamiliar ways. The latter can often cause the
  most confusion.</p>

<p>Once we have a list of specialized vocabulary we can ask the agent to
  produce a glossary, with definitions for each term. Some terms may
  be used in different ways, in which case the LLM should provide alternative
  definitions, with a note as to what context the differences appear in. The LLM
  can be asked to follow the output format of a dictionary, with a focus on
  precision and conciseness.</p>

<p>Once we have a draft glossary, then  human experts in that domain should
  review it, and amend it for cases where the LLM has got the wrong ends of
  sticks.</p>

<p>We then put the glossary somewhere where any other LLM can use it. It
  should be in a form that's easy for the LLM to consult, and minimize the
  tokens the LLM should consume in order to find meaning. We should set up a
  schedule to review and refresh the glossary at regular intervals, by agents
  and humans. We should keep it in version control, so we can see how it changes
  over time.</p>

<p>Large organizations often have subtly different definitions in different
  places, essentially following the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/DomainDrivenDesign.html">Domain Driven Design</a> notion
  of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/BoundedContext.html">Bounded Context</a>. Source code can be a particularly
  interesting bounded context, allowing folks to understand how terminology is
  used within a code base. Sometimes a single glossary can span several of
  bounded contexts, but at other times it's better to have separate glossaries
  for each one, allowing LLMs to consult whichever glossaries that are relevant
  to what they are doing. I'd urge everyone to resist any temptation to create a
  single, standard, glossary - I've yet to see that work. Often the value of a
  glossary isn't to nail down a single definition of a term, but instead to
  highlight its different usage in different contexts.</p>

<p>When there are different bounded contexts it can be interesting to compare
  different glossaries, or to get LLMs to compare glossaries and describe
  differences in usage. Comparing a source-code derived glossary to a human text
  can be particularly instructive.</p>

<p>If the glossary is large, it can be worthwhile to only include the terms
  and links to definitions as part of the initial context. The model can then
  use the links for only the full definitions it needs while working.</p>

<p>A glossary can be used in several ways within <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/AgenticEngineering.html">Agentic Engineering</a>. Glossaries can inform generation of code, so that
  names within the generated software fit the particular vocabulary of the
  enterprise. <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/harness-engineering.html">Sensors in a
  harness</a> can check for this usage, both in the internals of the software
  and in the user-interface.</p>

<p>When generating code or documents, we may introduce new bounded contexts.
  For example, internal names that may be used within an organization should not
  be exposed in a customer-facing UI, because such internal terms are likely to be
  only confuse a customer. In this case a customer has a different bounded
  context to the part of the enterprise serving them.</p>

<p>It's often useful for glossaries to include terms that should not be used,
  in which case it includes advice on what other term to use instead. Terms that
  are deprecated can be handled in this way, with glossary entries that indicate
  what period of time a term should be in effect.</p>

<p>In my conversations with people working with agents in large organizations,
  a common theme is the difficulty of specialized language. This encourages the
  activities of domain modeling, building ontologies, and constructing explicit
  <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/making-data-ready-for-agentic-ai.html#context-layer">context
  layers in enterprise data</a>. Getting LLMs to generate a glossary is a
  simple, but useful, part of this work.</p>

<div class="acknowledgements">
<h2>Acknowledgements</h2>

<p>Eric Nagler and Unmesh Joshi related their experiences doing this
    activity and thus inspired this post. Amy Pellegrini, Annie Luxton, Arun Srinivasan, Chris Ford, Daniel
    Terhorst-North, Dave Farley, Eric Nagler, James Shore, Jason Yip, Jimmy Nilsson, Matteo Vaccari, Michaël Le Barbier, Pete Hodgson, Pramod
    Sadalage, Ravi Vats, Unmesh Joshi, and Valentyn Logvynskyi commented on drafts.
    </p>
</div>
