### [Tests as data: ensuring AI-generated code does what we want](https://martinfowler.com/articles/tests-as-data.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/tests-as-data.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/tests-as-data/card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p>Automated testing in the age of AI coding assistants is problematic:
     when AI agents write both production and test code, developers risk losing
     the assurance that automated tests protect against functional errors.
     <b class="author">Matteo Vaccari</b> makes three claims: (1) when AI writes the
     code, developers become its customers; (2) therefore customer tests, not
     developer tests, become the primary contract between humans and AI; (3)
     moving that contract into test data rather than test code makes it easier
     to protect it from unexpected modifications by coding agents. All of this
     optimizes for human-readable tests that establish a contract between humans
     and AI. While in the past DSL parsers and custom test runners were
     expensive to write and maintain, now AI makes them cheap.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/tests-as-data.html">more…</a></p>

---

### [Fragments: November 10](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-11-10.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>It’s that time of the year when the <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/radar">November Thoughtworks Technology Radar</a> appears. Again my colleagues have taken a sweep of the technologies that our teams have used in the field, and pulled together a summary of what’s popped up in the last six months. It’s been a busy half-year, one that again has been dominated by AI, and that’s reflected in the many AI blips on the radar.</p>

<p>The mass of blips makes it hard to develop a coherent story around trends, but Chris Ford has done a fine job of the writing the <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/insights/blog/technology-strategy/macro-trends-tech-industry-november-2026">accompanying article on macro trends</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>One team of passionate people experimenting with agents can make almost anything work. They share a context, a credit card and a mental model. They review each other’s work because they sit together, and they know what to check and how much latitude to give the coding agent.</p>

  <p>Two hundred people, though, cannot work that way. The moment agentic AI becomes an operating model rather than a pilot, a second layer of problems appears: How is context shared between teams? What’s the token bill? How do we deal with increased review demands? What’s the agent allowed to reach, read and change? And who’s now responsible for the harness everyone depends on?</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Experience so far suggests that the questions that enterprises face are about: shared context, token cost, cognitive load, security, and platform building. While these issues are distinct, they are not separate, so strategies to answer them must take all of them into account. I also echo Chris in reminding us that however impressive these tools are, we must remember that while we have to rethink systems and workflows to utilize these technologies, they should be built to serve humans - and we should all benefit from its bounty.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Ben Thompson’s Stratechery newsletter <a href="https://stratechery.com/">reflects on  the recent election’s impact on data centers</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>I watch a lot of sports, and particularly this fall, watched a lot of baseball. My Milwaukee Brewers — excuse me, my World Champion Milwaukee Brewers — demanded my full attention, which also meant I saw a lot of campaign ads, and by far the most common topic, at least here in Wisconsin, was data centers. Moreover, it was never entirely clear which way it cut politically: both parties were anti-data center, accusing their opponent of being the opposite.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He points out that this opposition is recent, and tied to a shift in focus from the internet and streaming movies to AI:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>you know, the technology that in the best case scenario is going to take everyone’s jobs, and in the worst case scenario end the human race.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>There’s going to be a lot said about how Democratic officials will be at the heart of blocking data center building, but as his point above about campaign ads illustrates, the opposition to data centers is (at least at the moment) a bi-partisan opposition.</p>

<p>He follows up with “ten takes” from these consequences. The big beneficiaries of this opposition to building data centers will be those who own the data centers that have already been built. The opposition will reduce supply of tokens, meaning that token costs will go up, thus increasing the profits for those who already have their token generators plugged in and humming.</p>

<p>There’s also benefits to those who aren’t constrained by the need to build more big data centers. Other countries aren’t affected by a change in US policy, China is the most talked-about case here, but it’s not the only country that may benefit from a drop in US data center building. Apple may also benefit, if it means more AI work is pushed to edge devices that are their territory, in addition to having not sunk so many dollars into AI-specific investments.</p>

<p>To his analysis, I’ll add that this may also have a somewhat counter-intuitive effect on regulation, and corporate attitudes to it. The common expectation is that businesses are opposed to government regulation, since that interferes with their free pursuit of profit. That’s true to an extent, but it’s quite common to see established businesses lobby (quietly) in favor of regulation that acts as a barrier to new competitors. So I wouldn’t be at all surprised to see companies that have already built data centers to be pushing for regulations that impede or prevent new ones from being built.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I’ve been a long-time fan of Hadley Wickham, for how he has tamed the rather messy world of R programming into a <a href="https://tidyverse.org/">well-designed tidyverse</a>. He is another thoughtful voice to listen to as we move into the world of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/AgenticEngineering.html">agentic engineering</a>, in this case by trying to figure out <a href="https://substack.hadley.nz/p/how-much-do-tokens-really-cost">what the real cost of tokens is</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>I hear a lot of suspicion that AI tokens are unsustainably priced and that frontier labs are heavily subsidising costs with their VC money. They want to get us hooked now, so they can pull the rug out from under us whenever they want to make a bunch of money. Is that true? My sense is that it’s mostly not. But can I back that up with some data?</p>
</blockquote>

<p>His conclusion is that it doesn’t seem that token costs are significantly subsidized on per-token API basis, but they are with subscription plans. (He caveats his analysis, saying it doesn’t take into account recouping the costs of training the models.)</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>There’s a creepy excitement at the thought that models are clever enough to edit the weights of models, an activity that <a href="https://rikiyat.github.io/blog/weight-space-programming/">Takehi et al refer to as Weight-Space Programming</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Just by looking at the raw weights, GPT-6 Astra can tell what a network was trained to do. And without any environment feedback, it has no trouble writing, from scratch, small game-playing networks that outplay networks trained with PPO for 100 million steps.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>While we don’t know if we’ll ever be able to download skills directly into our brains (<a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fhrNgXJ__n8">“I know kung fu”</a> - or for those in my particular demographic: <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_90">Joe 90</a>), this does raise the possibility of downloading capabilities into models by manipulating their weights.</p>

<p>Simon Wilison riffed on this paper by <a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Nov/3/qwen-can-interpret-model-weights/">examining whether an LLM can understand model behaviors from the weights alone</a>, by examining four simple neural networks, each trained to play a specific game, and figuring out which game it was by just looking at the weights.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/jessitron/status/2117400669044793405">Jessica Kerr (jessitron):</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>My days of teaching AI to do stuff are numbered.</p>

  <p>Getting it to teach me stuff is more fun.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Houck et al (that “al” includes some folks who I’ve learned to pay a lot of attention to), look at how we can assess the quality of context supplied to agents.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>An agent may draw from source code, specifications, issue trackers, chat histories, architectural documents, tool output, and user instructions, then act on that combination with limited human supervision. Information that was written for different audiences, at different times, and for different purposes is brought together into a temporary operating environment for a particular task. Whether each source is individually useful does not reveal whether the resulting context is fit for the work the agent is being asked to do.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>They’ve come up with a <a href="https://queue.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3847288">framework for describing the quality of this assembled context</a>, which they refer to as CAFE(S). The acronym refers to five dimensions they look at:</p>

<ul>
  <li>Clarity: Can the agent interpret the request as intended?</li>
  <li>Actionability: Can the agent proceed, and know when it is done?</li>
  <li>Fidelity: Can the agent trust that this context is true?</li>
  <li>Efficiency: Can the agent focus on what matters?</li>
  <li>Security: Should the agent have this context at all?</li>
</ul>

<p>They also list a set of half-a-dozen “context smells”: common cases where these break down (and a nod to Kent’s <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/CodeSmell.html">original metaphor</a>)</p>

<p>I’m not sure about the name, (how exactly should I pronounce “CAFE(S)”?) and the article exhibits rather more <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/LLM-Voice.html">LLM Voice</a> that I prefer. But on the whole I think this is a good framing for thinking about an important part of feeding our silicon servants/overlords.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Cindy Sridharan noted that <a href="https://x.com/copyconstruct/status/2118529738532925725">the problems she ran into six months ago are no longer around</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>What was missing in my setup 6 months ago was the verification process I now use.</p>

  <p>70% of my daily token spend goes into verifying patches, and only ~20-30% into creating them.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>That resonated with conversations I’ve had with folks using agents in enterprise settings. They are using far more tokens to check the working of models (through guardrails and evals) than they are to generate a response to a request. A single request to a model from a customer may trigger a dozen turns to other models to probe its input and verify its output.</p>

---

### [Long-horizon agents and durable execution](https://martinfowler.com/articles/durable-execution-agents.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/durable-execution-agents.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/durable-execution-agents/card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p>Long-horizon agents can automate complex tasks that take days or weeks
     to complete and involve multiple exchanges with people and external
     systems. Many things can go wrong during this time, including process
     crashes, network failures, and unanticipated real-world behavior.
     <b class="author">Kiran Prakash</b> explains durable execution: an emerging
     pattern that helps agents recover from these failures and move towards
     their goal. In this programming model, the runtime keeps a record of the
     result of each step that interacts with the outside world so that, when a
     failure occurs, the program runs again, completed steps are skipped, and
     the program's state is rebuilt from the record.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/durable-execution-agents.html">more…</a></p>

---

### [The Data Product Platform Navigator](https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-product-platform-nav.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-product-platform-nav.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-product-platform-nav/card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p>Many organizations adopting data mesh and data products face a common
     challenge. They may be tasked with building a self-service platform for
     data products and data mesh from scratch, or they may have an existing
     platform that suffers from low adoption, preventing their data mesh from
     scaling. This article provides a platform navigator, based on the CNCF's
     Platform Engineering Maturity Model, to help you reason about your current
     situation and determine the next steps for your platform.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-product-platform-nav.html">more…</a></p>

### [Bliki: LLM Voice](https://martinfowler.com/bliki/LLM-Voice.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Sometimes when I read a post, I get the growing feeling that I'm reading
  the output of an LLM. I can't necessarily define why, although there are the common
  tells (it's not the em-dashes, it's the load-bearing tapestries). As I
  do this my desire to read on atrophies, sometimes to the point of revulsion</p>

<p>There are tools out there that claim to be able to tell the difference
  between LLM-written prose and the old-fashioned biological product. I'm wary
  of them, partly due skeptism about their accuracy, but also because it rather
  misses the point. The reaction to LLM-Voice is a human one, that sense that we
  are in an <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncanny_valley">uncanny
  valley</a> of prose. By the time I'd reach for a tool to tell me what I'm
  reading, it's already too late, even if it confidently tells me there's no LLM
  present.</p>

<p>I'm also suspicious of my own ability to accurately recognize LLM voice. Language
  changes over time, so a common sign of age is complaining about the language
  of the youngs. I'm inclined to bristle at prose that talks about “adopting a
  new sales <i>motion</i>,” or “a <i>sharp</i> round-up of news”. But I've heard
  these constructs coming out of human mouths, so that's more a generational
  difference rather than between carbon and silicon. (And as a transplanted Brit,
  don't get me started on Americanisms, I'm sure I'll get used to them momentarily.)</p>

<p>Right now I urge authors to be extra-wary of LLM-Voice. In particular I
  strongly recommend that authors should not fall into the trap of getting the
  LLM to write a draft. Even if it's edited by a human, it's very hard to expel
  the LLM-Voice - thus causing the uncanny valley reaction. 
<sup>1</sup>
 Even accepting suggestions of rephrasing is dangerous. I
  have heard of people using LLMs to develop outline and structure, that
  <i>may</i> be ok, but only as far as an outline. 
<sup>2</sup>

  Authors should fill the prose themselves. </p>

<p><a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/SayYourWriting.html">Say Your Writing</a> is
  even more important in the age of LLMs. I habitually speak the words in my head
  and then write them down (and then edit what I've just written). This advice
  is particularly important to anyone who has any desire to communicate
  regularly with writing. Like many things, learning to be a good writer relies
  on lots of practice. It's a skill worth the time to acquire, but surrendering
  to an LLM will undermine any progress.</p>

<p>In time it may be that LLMs will be so good that we can't tell the
  difference. In many ways, that's more worrying. Like most writers, I find
  writing an almost-essential tool for thinking. Often it's only by writing
  something down, that I can really get my thoughts on a topic into coherence.
  I've often started an essay knowing I don't really understand something, but
  the act of wrestling with the words forces me to overcome my confusions. As
  more people delegate writing to LLMs they will also delegate thinking and
  comprehension to LLMs. I don't want to do that, where's the fun of writing then?</p>

<p>Although avoiding LLM-Voice is a general rule, it isn't an absolute one. An
  important example is that of Jim Highsmith (who contributes to this site). He
  finds working with an LLM is essential to his writing to <a href="https://bjsm.bmj.com/content/60/9/677">overcome the bane of Parkinson's
  disease</a>. I'll gladly deal with LLM-Voice in order to continue imbibing my
  old friend's wisdom.</p>

<p>A more awkward case is that of translation. Many of my colleagues aren't
  comfortable in English, it is their second or fourth language. Some have found
  LLM translation an important part to their communication with those of us who
  know rather less Marathi. But I also would prefer rather a genuine human's clunky prose to the
  smooth but soulless LLM.</p>

<p>There are situations where it would be reasonable for even a native
  Englishman to let LLMs write the prose. Such as if I'm writing a form letter
  to an official, or preparing a report I suspect no-one will ever read 
<sup>3</sup>
. Maybe
  most documentation in the future will only be read by LLMs, and they won't
  have my uncanny valley worries. But if I want to communicate with humans, my
  humanity has to shine (or at least ooze) out of my prose.</p>

<div class="footnote-list">
<h2>Notes</h2>

<div class="footnote-list-item" id="footnote-impossible">
<p><span class="num">1: </span>
      Very hard, but not impossible. I've come across one case where I do know
      the first draft was entirely done by LLM, but I detected no LLM Voice when
      I read the final piece. It did, however, need a professional editor at a
      major periodical publication to clean it up.
    </p>
</div>

<div class="footnote-list-item" id="footnote-policy">
<p><span class="num">2: </span>
      My policy on this site, is that anyone who uses AI to help them write
       should declare how they used it, usually in the
      acknowledgments. This is partly for transparency, and partly so that
      readers can learn useful ways to use AI. In my own case, I don't use AI
      for generating prose, nor for help in structure. The only way I use it for
      writing is as a search tool (but I verify any information by going
      to the source).
    </p>
</div>

<div class="footnote-list-item" id="footnote-official">
<p><span class="num">3: </span>
      Michael Nygard commented: “There you really aren't
      trying to communicate thoughts from one person to other people. You're
      communicating with a process rather than a human. A human may open the
      envelope but that person is acting as a component in a cybernetic
      organism.”
    </p>
</div>
</div>

<div class="acknowledgements">
<h2>Acknowledgements</h2>

<p>Alessio Ferri, Caer Sanders, Ian Cartwright, Jie Jessie Xia, Joshua
    Kerievsky, Michael Feathers, Michael Nygard, Scott Davies, and Wei Zhang commented on
    drafts of this post.</p>

<p>I did not use AI or LLMs in any way while writing this post.</p>
</div>

### [Why are good developers so wary of agentic development?](https://martinfowler.com/rachels-ramblings/good-devs-wary.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p class="precis"><b>TL;DR</b><br /><i>Good developers’ caution about agentic development comes from knowing that built isn’t the same as working in production.</i></p>
<img src="https://martinfowler.com/rachels-ramblings/card.png" /><p>I’ve been surprised by how many excellent developers have been hesitant to go all in on coding agents. These are people who usually try new ways of working before anyone else and in many cases invent new tools and practices that support building the application well. So why have we had so much reluctance?</p>

<p>The easy (lazy?) answer is that they love writing code, or they’re worried about their jobs. I’m sure that’s true for some people, some of the time. And I know it is true for some of the developers I talk to but I always felt there was something else, another fear hiding behind those obvious ones. When we put together the <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/en-us/radar">Technology Radar</a> every six months I try to block out a chunk of time for us to do an open space. In September, a discussion we had on agentic development, which pushed it to its limits, finally helped me see clearly what that fear is.</p>

<h2 id="building-it-right">Building it right</h2>

<p>Thoughtworkers care deeply about <em>building the right thing and building it right</em>. It’s pretty much our default way of working and thinking and it’s where many of the practices and technologies we invented and championed came from. We want software that is useful and helpful, can be changed next year, and doesn’t fall over in production. Our developers adopted, championed or invented some of the first versions of continuous integration, pairing, automated testing, continuous delivery, microservices and many others early because they helped us build the software right.</p>

<p>Early coding agents made it very easy to produce code, including code we wouldn’t have wanted anywhere near production, written by people who have not learned about the chasm between building something and making it run in production safely. The tools have improved enormously, but experienced developers still have questions. Are we confident this is the right behaviour? Does it work as expected? Are these the right boundaries? Will we be able to evolve it? Does it abide by the principles and practices we learned the hard way to give us confidence it will meet the needs of the customer and stay up in production? What happens when something fails? Will another team be able to live with it? And so on.</p>

<p>Underneath all of those sits a bigger question. <strong>How much can we trust a system when nobody has experience of building it this way?</strong> Let me state that another way. Nearly every system we have in production now was built the old way, by hand, by people we could trust (hopefully). Everything we build now is the new way and we have not lived with that yet.</p>

<p>After more than 25 years in technology, I’ve come to value the judgment people develop through experience. I regularly put similar questions to models and to senior technologists at Thoughtworks. For the difficult questions, I still get more useful answers from the humans or maybe more insightful with very specific experience that helps me better understand the point. They challenge the premise, recognise a pattern from something that went wrong ten years ago, or spot the organisational problem hiding inside the technical one. I can now access an extraordinary amount of information. I still need people who can help me think through what matters, I still need wisdom and insight. That type judgment is exactly what hesitant experienced developers are trying to protect.</p>

<p>And I want to be clear: they are not hesitant to use a coding assistant, they are hesitant to go all in and let the agents do all the execution. They are hesitant to take their hand off the wheel because they don’t have full confidence the agents have the right guardrails in place to run off on their own.</p>

<h2 id="a-week-at-the-whiteboard">A week at the whiteboard</h2>

<p>Recently, Giles Alexander, our Regional CTO for Europe, brought senior engineers, infrastructure consultants and product people together for a week of working in exactly this way. No code written by anyone. Everything built by agents. The point wasn’t to prove that an airline application could be built in a week. (Funnily enough they got 80% done and we all know what that means… you can’t make stuff up!) The purpose of the experiment was to learn what happens to the team’s work when agents do all of the coding. You can read some of what they learned <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/exploring-gen-ai/an-accidental-blackboard.html">here</a>.</p>

<p>What struck me in our conversations was how much time they spent at the whiteboard. Working out the domain. Drawing boundaries. Deciding what should happen next and making plans the agents could act on. Meanwhile, the agents kept producing code beyond the team’s working day.</p>

<p>The humans were also designing the feedback around that work: tests, contracts, architecture checks and a delivery pipeline. They were working out how to give agents enough freedom to make progress while keeping confidence in the result. Most of the work at the keyboard was directing agents to put this learning into the harness, so the building and testing agents got it right on the next run and they could have more confidence in what they built. Interestingly in the end the product person committed more feature code than anyone else on the team.</p>

<p>When I asked them to estimate they said they spend about ⅔ of their time at the whiteboard and ⅓ on the harness, which could give us some clue as to how developers will work in the future, but before you get ahead of yourself redesigning your team, that’s not the end of the story…</p>

<h2 id="the-application-ran-the-build-was-red">The application ran. The build was red.</h2>

<p>The application ran, but the build was frequently red. It worked on their machine - again you couldn’t make this up if you tried! A constant red build gives me the ick. A red build should mean something: stop, investigate, fix it. If it’s red so often that people stop paying attention, how do we spot the next real failure? And if we can’t trust the checks, a green build stops being reassuring too. That’s exactly the uncertainty <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/continuousIntegration.html">continuous integration</a> was supposed to remove, and we’ve been advocating it for over 25 years.</p>

<p>We’re still working through how to address it. Remember this was an experiment to push the very edge of agentic development. Were too many agents working in parallel and stepping on each other’s changes? Was the system not yet modular enough for agents to work on separate parts? Or were changes simply arriving faster than the checks could finish? Or was it a combination of all of these and more? Either way we definitely need to rethink how we will solve the last mile challenges. I can see continuous delivery/DevOps as a movement having a resurgence and a rethink in the agentic era in the same way the rest of the development life cycle is being upended. And it isn’t just our experiment. <a href="https://cloud.google.com/blog/products/ai-machine-learning/announcing-the-2025-dora-report?utm_source=chatgpt.com">DORA’s 2025</a> research found AI adoption is now linked to higher delivery throughput, but to lower delivery stability. Exactly what continuous delivery and DevOps were trying to solve for!</p>

<p>All of these challenges prompted some good arguments internally. What role should test-driven development play when you aren’t reading every line and the agent created the tests alongside the code? Where would acceptance tests, contract tests and checks at system boundaries give us more confidence without being too brittle? How much design should we do before setting an agent loose, given how quickly we can revise an approach? Is there an appropriate point we will know we have gone too far? One of the biggest puzzles in all this is the size of the batch. I have heard many teams are moving from Stories to Epics. Great for speed of development but can your CI pipeline handle that size unit of work at speed and in parallel?</p>

<p>The experiment team didn’t settle all of this in a week or even two (they ran it twice with different teams). I wouldn’t trust anyone who claimed they had. But this is exactly the kind of work I want experienced developers involved in: experimenting, questioning and helping us discover which practices hold up and where we need some new ones.</p>

<h2 id="off-the-groomed-slopes">Off the groomed slopes</h2>

<p>After coming away from this conversation with some of the senior developers involved I kept thinking about learning to ski. Kids will often head straight down the hill without fear, kind of like a new builder who has never put software into production and has never fixed an on-call issue at 3am (something I will never forget, right up there with every production issue I personally caused and the resultant learnings from those, which are burned in my brain forever). When I first tried to ski as an adult, I was thinking about what I might break. I wanted to ski, it looked so fun and freeing, but I was old enough to see the consequences and I didn’t fancy hobbling around London with a broken limb or worse. My biggest issue was I hadn’t yet learned how to turn or stop effectively and without that I was a runaway train on a ski slope.</p>

<p>When I shared a draft of this post with Martin, who also learned to ski late, he pointed out where the comparison breaks down (like all analogies I like to push them to the edge, pun intended). On a ski slope you’ve watched countless people reach the bottom with no broken bones. The instructors have taught thousands of people before you on the same slope. They know which skills matter and how to teach them. Agentic development right now is more like a backcountry trip across unknown territory. We don’t yet know which skills will matter most. Not many people, if any, have finished the trip safely and the feedback on how to navigate it is slow or non-existent. As Martin put it, we won’t know whether a heavily agent-built system works until it has run in production for a while, survived a few rounds of change, and not sent all our planes to Aberystwyth. <strong>Built is not the same as working in production.</strong> Or as Charity Majors <a href="https://x.com/mipsytipsy/status/1822877010894758096">puts it</a>, “staging is just a glorified laptop. Only prod is prod”. Which is why when someone says “it works on my machine” it’s usually intended as a joke. Or at least I always laugh.</p>

<p>That solidified a nagging discomfort I have had with leaders, technical or non-technical, claiming devs aren’t curious, or are laggards slow to adopt, and putting that down to easy-to-point-at fears like missing writing code (I do too) and fearing for our jobs (who isn’t right now?). Our best developers aren’t nervous beginners on a nursery slope that everyone else has already mastered. They’re looking at unmarked terrain and asking sensible questions about the route. Telling them to be less cautious misses the point. They need to be on the team that maps it.</p>

<p>Of course, experience can also make us too attached to familiar practices. Some of what we learned on the groomed slopes won’t hold out there, and we need to be willing to test our assumptions.</p>

<h2 id="a-challenge-for-both-sides">A challenge for both sides</h2>

<p>So there’s a challenge here for both leaders and developers. Leaders need to listen carefully enough to tell useful concerns from reluctance to change, and to create real room for experimentation. That means time, permission to get things wrong, and not judging success by how much code the agents produced. Developers need to bring their experience into those experiments and be prepared to find that some of their assumptions no longer hold.</p>

<p>Some familiar practices will become more important. Others will need a different shape. Nobody is going to hand us a map first.</p>

<p>If you care deeply about building the right thing and building it right, your judgment is exactly what this new way of working needs. Bring the caution. Then let’s set off into the snow.</p>

---

## LLM Infrastructure

### [v0.34.0](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/releases/tag/v0.34.0)

**来源**: vLLM GitHub Releases

**摘要**: <h1>v0.34.0</h1>
<p><strong>1,061 commits from 412 contributors (120 new).</strong> Thank you!</p>
<h2>TL;DR</h2>
<ul>
<li><strong>10 new models</strong> (Mistral Large 4, MiniMax-M3.1-Flash, Nemotron-3.5-Transcribe, and more!)</li>
<li><strong>Extensible KV cache is on by default:</strong> vLLM sizes the KV cache from memory actually free after warmup, so most models get a bigger cache and more throughput - in most cases you should now <strong>remove <code>gpu_memory_utilization</code></strong> from your configs.</li>
<li><strong>Model Runner V1 is gone.</strong> Model Runner V2 is now the only GPU runner.</li>
<li><strong>Big speedups</strong> on Blackwell and MI355X: up to 1.95× decode (Step5 on GB200), 1.6× throughput (GLM-5.3-Flash), 1.24–1.40× faster long-prompt TTFT (GLM-5.3).</li>
<li><strong>Upgrade check:</strong> default block size is now 64, ROCm 7.2 images are gone, and <code>huggingface_hub &gt;= 2.0.0</code> is required. See <a href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/releases.atom#before-you-upgrade">Before you upgrade</a>.</li>
</ul>
<h2>Before you upgrade</h2>
<div class="markdown-alert markdown-alert-important"><p class="markdown-alert-title"><svg class="octicon octicon-report mr-2" height="16" version="1.1" viewBox="0 0 16 16" width="16" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg"><path d="M0 1.75C0 .784.784 0 1.75 0h12.5C15.216 0 16 .784 16 1.75v9.5A1.75 1.75 0 0 1 14.25 13H8.06l-2.573 2.573A1.458 1.458 0 0 1 3 14.543V13H1.75A1.75 1.75 0 0 1 0 11.25Zm1.75-.25a.25.25 0 0 0-.25.25v9.5c0 .138.112.25.25.25h2a.75.75 0 0 1 .75.75v2.19l2.72-2.72a.749.749 0 0 1 .53-.22h6.5a.25.25 0 0 0 .25-.25v-9.5a.25.25 0 0 0-.25-.25Zm7 2.25v2.5a.75.75 0 0 1-1.5 0v-2.5a.75.75 0 0 1 1.5 0ZM9 9a1 1 0 1 1-2 0 1 1 0 0 1 2 0Z"></path></svg>Important</p><p>These changes can break existing setups.</p>
</div>
<table>
<thead>
<tr>
<th>Change</th>
<th>What to do</th>
<th>PR</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>Model Runner V1 removed</td>
<td>Remove <code>VLLM_USE_V2_MODEL_RUNNER=0</code>; it now fails at startup. Port V1 logits processors to <code>vllm.v1.worker.gpu.sample.logits_processor</code>.</td>
<td><a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/63373">#63373</a></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Default <code>--block-size</code> is 64 (was 16)</td>
<td>Pass <code>--block-size 16</code> to keep the old behavior. ROCm paged attention still uses 16.</td>
<td><a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/63231">#63231</a></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Extensible KV cache on by default; <code>gpu_memory_utilization</code> defaults to 1.0</td>
<td><strong>In most cases it's now best to not set <code>gpu_memory_utilization</code></strong>. This should provide maximum kv cache capacity while avoiding runtime OOMs. Use it to share a GPU with other processes or when extensible kv cache is disabled.</td>
<td><a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56492">#56492</a></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>ROCm 7.2 artifacts dropped</td>
<td>Use the ROCm 10.0 wheels and images. <code>-rocm72</code> is no longer published.</td>
<td><a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/61939">#61939</a></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><code>huggingface_hub &gt;= 2.0.0</code> required</td>
<td>Also requires <code>tokenizers &gt;= 0.23.3</code> and <code>timm &gt;= 1.0.30</code>.</td>
<td><a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/64779">#64779</a></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>QuTLASS / <code>fp_quant</code> removed</td>
<td><code>fp_quant</code> checkpoints no longer load.</td>
<td><a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/65230">#65230</a></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Marlin W4A8 removed</td>
<td>Use <code>--quantization humming</code> with <code>VLLM_HUMMING_INPUT_QUANT_CONFIG</code> instead of <code>VLLM_MARLIN_INPUT_DTYPE</code>.</td>
<td><a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/65542">#65542</a></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Keye-VL and Keye-VL-1.5 removed</td>
<td>—</td>
<td><a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/64287">#64287</a></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>All breaking changes, deprecations and new defaults are listed in <a href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/releases.atom#full-changelog">the full changelog</a>.</p>

<h2>Highlights</h2>
<h3>Extensible KV cache, on by default</h3>
<ul>
<li>On CUDA and ROCm, the KV cache is sized from memory actually free <strong>after</strong> CUDA graph capture and warmup, not from an estimate (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56492">#56492</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Result:</strong> a larger cache (e.g. MiniMax-M3 on H200: 433k → 672k tokens), so throughput is higher and TTFT lower at high concurrency.</li>
<li><strong>Trade-off:</strong> per-token decode latency can rise on some models (TPOT +6–18% on some H200 models at concurrency 128). Opt out with <code>--gpu-memory-utilization 0.9</code> or <code>--no-enable-extensible-kv-cache</code>.</li>
<li>Hardened with fixes for allocator fragmentation, startup OOMs and KV connectors (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/67296">#67296</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/67485">#67485</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/66731">#66731</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/66523">#66523</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/66197">#66197</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/65137">#65137</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/66605">#66605</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/66674">#66674</a>).</li>
</ul>
<h3>Model Runner V2 only</h3>
<ul>
<li>Model Runner V1 is removed (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/63373">#63373</a>).</li>
<li>Dual-batch overlap now runs on MRV2, including with speculative decoding and DCP (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/61464">#61464</a>).</li>
</ul>
<h3>Speculative decoding</h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Suffix decoding</strong> runs on MRV2 with a GPU speculator; Arctic Inference is no longer needed (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/60339">#60339</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Custom speculators:</strong> declarative configs plus a <code>register_speculative_method</code> plugin API (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/63205">#63205</a>).</li>
<li><strong>CUDA graphs</strong> for MRV2 draft-model speculators (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/60989">#60989</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Adaptive verification</strong> works with XPress (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/65175">#65175</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Draft KV cache matches the target's dtype</strong>, so an FP8 target gets an FP8 draft cache (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/65198">#65198</a>).</li>
</ul>
<h3>Performance</h3>
<table>
<thead>
<tr>
<th>Improvement</th>
<th>Hardware</th>
<th>Gain</th>
<th>PR</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>Step5 NVFP4 MoE on TRTLLM-Gen</td>
<td>4×GB200</td>
<td>up to <strong>1.95×</strong> decode</td>

</tr>
<tr>
<td>GLM-5.3-Flash DeepGEMM FP8 MegaMoE</td>
<td>NVIDIA</td>
<td><strong>1.6×</strong> output throughput vs DEP4</td>

</tr>
<tr>

</tr>
<tr>
<td>Token-sharded sparse-MLA prefill under TP (default)</td>
<td>GB200</td>

<td><a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/63956">#63956</a></td>
</tr>

<h3>AMD ROCm</h3>
<ul>
<li>Release images and wheels are <strong>ROCm 10.0 only</strong> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/61939">#61939</a>).</li>
<li>MoRI EPv2 via <code>--all2all-backend mori</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/61361">#61361</a>).</li>
<li>Kimi-K3 specialized decode on MI355X, opt-in (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/61435">#61435</a>).</li>
<li>TRITON_ATTN is the default attention backend on RDNA3 and RDNA4 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/60418">#60418</a>).</li>
</ul>
<h3>Serving and operations</h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Checkpoint/restore</strong> (CRIU + <code>cuda-checkpoint</code>) through <code>/checkpoint/prepare</code>, <code>/checkpoint/status</code> and <code>/checkpoint/restore</code>, in both the Python and Rust frontends (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/62570">#62570</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Fault-tolerant DP+EP scale-down:</strong> dead ranks are removed and their experts redistributed without a restart (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/46370">#46370</a>).</li>
<li>Best-effort <strong>NUMA binding</strong> on by default (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/61258">#61258</a>).</li>
<li>Opt-in <strong>cache-aware admission</strong> ordering (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/54625">#54625</a>).</li>
<li>Rust frontend: stateless <strong>Responses API</strong> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/63902">#63902</a>) and <strong><code>n &gt; 1</code></strong> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/65625">#65625</a>).</li>
</ul>
<h2>Install</h2>
<p><strong>Python wheels</strong></p>
<table>
<thead>
<tr>
<th>Platform</th>
<th>Install</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>CUDA 13.0 (PyPI)</td>
<td><code>pip install vllm</code></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>CUDA 13.0 (uv)</td>
<td><code>uv pip install vllm --torch-backend=auto</code></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>ROCm 10.0</td>
<td><code>pip install vllm --extra-index-url https://wheels.vllm.ai/rocm/0.34.0/rocm100</code></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>XPU</td>
<td><code>uv pip install vllm --extra-index-url https://wheels.vllm.ai/0.34.0/xpu --extra-index-url https://download.pytorch.org/whl/xpu --index-strategy unsafe-best-match</code></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

<table>
<thead>
<tr>
<th>Platform</th>
<th>Image</th>
<th>Short alias</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>CUDA 13.0 (default)</td>
<td><code>vllm/vllm-openai:v0.34.0</code></td>
